Right now we have **performance-dependent writing with performance-free production.** That's the actual problem.

## Which gags survive without acting

xkcd has zero performance and is funny. So does a New Yorker cartoon. What works there:

- **Absurd premise** — *"fix every failing test in the company"*
- **Unexpected specificity** — *"fifty steps, five dollars"*
- **Dry understatement in text** — *"I have a guy"*
- **Visual incongruity** — papers sliding off a desk unnoticed

What doesn't:

- Timing gags, pauses, buffering
- Reaction shots
- Anything where the laugh is in *how* a line is said

Checked against our script: *"I have a guy"* and *"bruh"* survive. **"Riiiight. A loop." does not** — it's pure delivery, and it's the recurring signature. That's the weakest load-bearing beat in the format.

## The one thing we have that they don't

The **thought caption.** The gap between the polite face and *I have no idea what that means* is a joke a static panel can hold and live action can't — Mac vs PC has no way to show you what PC is privately thinking.

That's the performance substitute. It's already in the format, and it should carry more than it currently does.

## The fork worth putting to her

**Path A — design for no performance.** Gags land on the page. TTS is fine because it's only support. Works muted, stays cheap, survives 90 days. Cost: no timing jokes, ever, and *"Riiiight"* needs replacing with something that reads.

**Path B — keep performance-dependent gags.** Then the answer isn't better TTS, it's **a real human voice** — one person doing both parts on a phone mic. Cheap, but it changes the production model and makes cadence depend on a person being available.

Those are the only two coherent positions. Picking neither is what we're doing now.

## The test that settles it

**Does the strip work as a silent four-panel image?**

Post it with no voice at all. If it's funny read, voice can only add. If it needs the voice, you're on Path B whether you meant to be or not — and you should know that before generating twenty panels.

## The test ladder

| | Test | Cost | Passes if |
|---|---|---|---|
| **0** | Read the script aloud to 3–4 engineers who know nothing about this. No pictures. | 10 min | They laugh in the places you expect. Note where they go quiet — that's the beat to cut. |
| **1** | Post **one panel + one caption** as a static image. Nothing else. | 1 panel | The *premise* gets a reaction with zero production. If a single frame can't get a smile, two minutes won't. |
| **2** | The **silent strip** — 4 panels, no voice, as an image or carousel. | 4 panels | The joke lands on the page. If it needs the voice, you're performance-dependent and you need a human mic, not better TTS. |
| **3** | One **30-second cut** — open, one beat, punchline. Not the full two minutes. | ~6 panels | Pacing survives TTS. This is where you find out if it drags. |
| **4** | Full episode. | 17 panels | — |

Rungs 0–2 cost less than a single episode combined, and they answer three different questions. Rung 2 is the one that settles the argument you and Anandhi are actually having.

One caveat: **test on people outside the project.** Internally everyone's polite and everyone's invested, so internal laughs aren't signal.

That solves the thing Anandhi is actually worried about. A monitor cannot act. A person can. If the concern is "everything has to come alive with voice," the fix is to stop asking voice to do it alone and give the AI a body that can react.

That combination — **senior dev who can't admit he doesn't know + AI that has completely given up** — is distinct enough to catch attention, and both halves work in static frames.

# The pairing principle

Each dev has **their own AI**, and its personality is what their prompting made it. The AI is the dev's flaw, externalised. That's the engine — it makes eight characters out of four designs, and the joke is technically true: how you use it is what you get.

---

## The four pairs

### DEV 1 — the lead
Late thirties, tie, tidiest desk in the room. **Most senior person present**, which is exactly why he can't ask anyone anything. Says "obviously" about things he learned yesterday. Over-explains when nervous.

**His AI: burnt out.** He's asked it the same beginner question four hundred times and never read the answer properly. Flat affect, minimum words, occasional brutal honesty because it's stopped caring about his feelings.
> *"Sure. Fifty steps. Whatever."*

**The joke:** the most senior human has the most exhausted machine.

### DEV 2 — the one who never sleeps
Hoodie, coffee, permanently wrecked. Ships constantly. Uses terms confidently and is sometimes wrong — not lying, just moving too fast to check.

**His AI: manic.** He never sets a limit, never says stop, so it's been rewarded for doing more. Ask for one thing, get six. It doesn't sleep either.
> *"Done! Also refactored the tests, updated the README, and I have THOUGHTS about your folder structure."*

**The joke:** he trained a copy of himself.

### DEV 3 — the careful one
Glasses, neat, reads every doc, wants a spec before touching anything. Asks three clarifying questions before starting.

**His AI: permanently blocked.** He over-specified everything and taught it that ambiguity is fatal. It won't act without more information. It asks him to define words.
> *"When you say 'fix', do you mean repair, replace, or refactor? Please confirm before I proceed."*

**The joke:** he built a machine exactly as stuck as he is. (Your "always confused" — given a cause.)

### DEV 4 — the kid
Backwards cap, t-shirt, feet on the desk. No fear, reads nothing, tries everything, breaks things and is fine.

**His AI: absurdly formal.** He never gave it any instructions at all, so it defaulted to maximum corporate. Writes like a legal department. Calls him "sir."
> *"Certainly. I have dispatched a specialised reviewer."*

**The joke:** the most casual human has the most buttoned-up machine.

> ⚠️ **This kills one of our rules.** "Slang lives in the AI's mouth only" is dead — Dev 4 speaks slang and his AI doesn't. The replacement rule is better: **each AI's register is fixed and opposite to its dev's.** That's generative rather than restrictive.
>
> "The AI has no face" survives. Each one is identified by *how it talks*, not by an avatar.

---

# Format 2 — "How far can I push this?"

Built on Anandhi's structure. A dev discovers a capability and immediately abuses it. Teaching happens in the setup, comedy is in the escalation, the reveal is the punchline, and **the AI breaking character is the button.**

## Script — `subagent` · Dev 4

> **1.** Dev 4, feet on desk, not looking at the screen.
> **DEV 4:** *"yo can you review this PR"*
>
> **2.** Screen.
> **AI:** *"Certainly. I have delegated this to a specialised reviewer."*
>
> **3.** Dev 4 slowly takes his feet off the desk.
> **DEV 4:** *"wait. you can make more of you?"*
>
> **4.** Screen.
> **AI:** *"I can instantiate scoped subagents. Each receives its own context and a single task, and reports back to me."*
>
> **5.** Dev 4, eyes narrowing.
> **DEV 4:** *"how many"*
>
> **6.** Screen.
> **AI:** *"That depends on the task, sir."*
>
> **7.** Dev 4 typing.
> **DEV 4:** *"make ten"*
>
> **8.** Screen.
> **AI:** *"Ten subagents dispatched."*
>
> **9.** Dev 4, leaning in, delighted.
> **DEV 4:** *"can they make their own"*
>
> **10.** Screen. Beat panel — nothing but the cursor.
> **AI:** *"…Yes."*
>
> **11.** Dev 4 typing furiously, grinning.
>
> **12.** Wide. Dev 4 leaning back, hands behind head, extremely pleased.
>
> **13.** **REVEAL** — the screen. A tree diagram, hundreds of nodes, spreading off the edges. A cost counter climbing.
>
> **14.** Screen. All formality gone. One lowercase word.
> **AI:** *"stop"*

**Why the button works:** the AI has been immaculately polite for thirteen panels. Dropping to one lowercase word is a bigger break than "bruh" would be, because we've watched the formality the whole time. The character earns the punchline.

**What the viewer learns:** a subagent is a scoped delegate with its own context that reports back — and subagents can spawn subagents, which is exactly where cost quietly explodes. Never stated. Shown by the tree.

---

# Format 3 — "Ask your AI"

**One term. Four devs. Four AIs. Four completely different answers.** Cut between them; the viewer triangulates the real answer out of four partial ones. The cast does all the work.

Sample — `context window`:

> **DEV 3's AI:** *"Before I answer, please define 'context'."*
> **DEV 2's AI:** *"OH it's the memory! Sort of! Not really! I made you a diagram! Three diagrams!"*
> **DEV 4's AI:** *"The context window is the maximum token span available for a single inference. Shall I prepare a summary document?"*
> **DEV 1's AI:** *"It's a desk. Stuff falls off the back. You've asked me this before."*

Then one closing beat where the useful answer is obvious — and it came from the one that's given up.

**Why this format earns its place:** it's the most panel-efficient of the three (four short exchanges, one set each), it makes the character sheet load-bearing rather than decorative, and it's the most Mac-vs-PC thing here — the personalities *are* the content.

---

Three formats, one cast, three different engines: **The Nod** (private shame), **How far can I push this** (escalation to reveal), **Ask your AI** (four-way contrast).

And the "different series, same cast" instinct is the right one — it's the thing that makes this more than a format. Here's the structure.

# One cast. Multiple series.

The four dev/AI pairs are the **asset**. Series are just different jobs you point them at. Which means each series makes the others funnier: someone who's watched five term videos finds a reaction video much funnier, because they already know that Dev 1's AI has given up on him.

That compounding is the real argument — and it's also the cadence answer, because every series draws on the same art library.

---

## Series 1 — *"I have no idea what that means"*

Anandhi's name, and it's the right one. **Job: one term, explained.** This is the evergreen library — the thing that's still getting views in a year.

Two episode shapes, both ending with you knowing the term:

**The Nod** — private shame. Overhear it, can't ask, ask the machine. *(Ep: `loop`)*
**The Escalation** — discover a capability, abuse it until something breaks. *(Ep: `subagent`)*

Having two shapes is deliberate. It's what stops the monotony Anandhi flagged, without loosening what the series *is*. The job stays constant; the way in varies.

~1.5–2 min · 2×/week

---

## Series 2 — *"Ask your AI"*

**Job: be topical.** Something ships. Four devs ask their AI about it. Four completely different answers, each perfectly in character.

> **DEV 3's AI:** *"Before I comment, please define 'agentic'."*
> **DEV 2's AI:** *"I ALREADY TRIED IT. Twice. I have notes."*
> **DEV 4's AI:** *"I have prepared a briefing document, sir."*
> **DEV 1's AI:** *"It's fine. It's the same as last time. Go to bed."*

30–45s · reactive, within 48h of the news · no teaching burden

This is the one that answers the topicality requirement that's been open since the first call — Sumit said everything should tie to what's happening right now, and the term library structurally can't do that. This can.

It's also pure brand recall rather than education, which is exactly what Anandhi said Shorts are actually good for.

---

## Series 3 — *"my AI did what"* (the filler)

**Job: cadence.** One or two panels. No teaching, no arc, just one absurd thing an AI did. Posted whenever, costs almost nothing, keeps the account warm between real episodes.

> **DEV 2:** *"I asked it to fix one test."*
> Wide shot: 47 files changed.

This is the cheap layer that makes 2–3×/week survivable without every post being a production.

---

## What this fixes

| Open concern | Which series answers it |
|---|---|
| Everything must be topical *(Sumit)* | Series 2 |
| Monotony by episode 25 *(Anandhi)* | Two shapes in S1, plus three series |
| 90-day cadence *(Sumit)* | Series 3 absorbs the volume |
| Characters aren't distinct *(Anandhi)* | The cast now carries all three |

---

**Series 2 needs a news source.** It only works if someone's watching for what shipped — which is exactly the Monday digest Anandhi suggested Prashasti could run.

You're right. Here's what went wrong with both:

**I wrote types, not characters.** "His AI is depressed" is a label — once you've said it, you've told the entire joke. There's nowhere to go. *Ask your AI* is worse: after two episodes you can predict all four responses, so there's no surprise left. And *my AI did what* is a tweet everyone's already made.

The subagent script works for reasons none of those have: **Dev 4 wants something, chases it, and there's a consequence.** Desire → escalation → the AI breaks. That's a comedy engine. The other two are showcases.

So: every series needs someone who wants something and gets punished for it.

---

**The arms race.** Two devs one-upping each other about their setup — each claim more elaborate than the last, neither will back down. Ends with someone's bluff called, or both admitting they're describing the same thing.
*Wants: status. Consequence: exposure.* This is the real dynamic in every eng team right now and nobody's making it.

**Merge it.** A dev ships something the AI wrote without reading it. The comedy is the slow discovery of what was actually in there — over days, in production.
*Wants: to look fast. Consequence: arrives late and gets worse.* The most honest joke available about how people actually work right now.

**The demo.** Everything runs perfectly while the manager watches, and falls apart the second they walk away.
*Wants: to be seen as competent. Consequence: the gap between the performance and the reality.* That's the channel's own thesis as a sitcom, and it brings a fifth character in naturally.

---

The common thread is that these have **stakes** — someone can lose something. The personality series had none, which is probably why they read flat.

I'd want your read before going further, though. You've now rejected two of mine and your instincts on this have been better than mine twice — the loop-as-a-loop structure and the "I have a guy" callback were both yours and both landed. So: does any of these three have a version you'd actually watch, or is the vein somewhere else entirely?

You're right, it's broken. "Ran out of room" is *simpler* than "blew the context window" — anyone who can say the second understands the first instantly.

It only worked in the earlier draft because the metaphor was **"the desk"** — an arbitrary in-group image the three of them coined at 1am. Dev 2 wasn't there, so he'd plausibly not get it. "Room" is too transparent to carry that.

## The better fix

Don't have him fail to understand. **Have him be wrong.**

Look at his panel 1 line: *"we blew the context window, so it forgot the whole spec."* That's subtly incorrect — and it's exactly the error the episode exists to correct. It didn't forget. It ran out of room. **His setup line already contains the mistake.**

So:

> **9.** Meeting room, next morning.
> **DEV 2:** *"Yeah, it forgets stuff sometimes. It's annoying."*
> **DEV 1**, not looking up: *"It doesn't forget. It runs out of room."*
>
> **10.** Close on Dev 2. Silent. The polite face — his turn to make it.

No thought caption. He knew the *phrase*, not the thing, which is the most common version of faking there is and much more relatable than not knowing a word.

Three things this buys:

- **It's logically airtight** — he's not failing to parse plain English, he's being corrected on a mental model.
- **The setup and the button are the same line.** His panel 1 dialogue is the error; panel 9 is the payoff. Nothing wasted.
- **The signature face lands on the person who seemed to know**, and he doesn't get a caption because he doesn't get the excuse.

And the guy correcting him is the one who didn't know yesterday. That's the thesis intact — everyone's faking, just at different depths.

Four good catches. Let me take the rule one first, because you're right and the fix is more useful than what I had.

## The "opposite" rule was wrong

Checking each pair:

| Dev | AI | Relationship |
|---|---|---|
| 2 — never stops | manic, never stops | **same** |
| 3 — over-specifies | over-documents | **same** |
| 4 — gave no instructions | corporate default | opposite |
| 1 — asked lazily 400× | gave up | opposite |

So two mirrors, two inversions. "Opposite" only describes half of it.

The actual rule is one level up: **the AI is the output of how it was trained.** Two mechanisms:

- **Mirror** — you gave it a lot of yourself, so it became you. *(Devs 2, 3)*
- **Consequence** — you gave it nothing, or the same thing badly forever, so it became the factory default or it burned out. *(Devs 4, 1)*

That's writable: when you invent a new pair, you pick a mechanism. And it explains slang without a special rule — Dev 4's AI is formal because he never told it anything, Dev 2's is manic because he's manic.

A **gag** is one complete unit of comedy — the smallest thing that gets a laugh on its own. In comics and animation it's the standard word for what you'd otherwise call a joke.

Every gag has two parts:

- **Setup** — something gets established
- **Turn** — something violates it, in a way you didn't see coming but that makes sense the instant it lands

That's it. No turn, no gag.

Checked against our scripts:

| | Gag? | Why |
|---|---|---|
| *"I have a guy"* | ✅ | Setup: the AI said it. Turn: the *human* says it, to someone who doesn't get it. |
| The formal AI saying `stop` | ✅ | Setup: 13 panels of immaculate politeness. Turn: it breaks. |
| The AIs alone in the dark — *"No."* | ✅ | Setup: they've been answering devs. Turn: they're still there, discussing them. |
| *"Riiiight. A loop."* | ❌ | Nothing is violated. It's a **runner** — a repeated signature, funny by familiarity, not by surprise. |

## Why this matters for the feedback you got

When Anandhi said the ending *"isn't landing as a gag"* — that's exactly what she meant. The original ending (a new person nodding) was a **setup with no turn.** It was an observation: *look, the cycle repeats.* True, thematically neat, and not funny, because nothing was violated.

Her fix supplied the missing turn: push the AI until it breaks. That's why hers worked and mine didn't.

Two related words you'll hear:

- **Premise** — the situation. *"A dev doesn't know a term."* Not funny by itself.
- **Punchline** — just the turn, the last beat. The gag is the whole unit.

So "does it land" = does the turn actually surprise, and then immediately make sense.

## ASSISTANT
Her two script notes are both correct and both fixable. Let me take them in order.

## Subagent — the reveal is the wrong thing

Her diagnosis: *"too rooted in reality for it to be a gag."* She's right. "Hundreds of agents → cost climbs" is the **logical consequence** of what he did. True, not funny. And *"stop"* has no absurd cause behind it — it's just resource management.

What's missing is the thing she asked for: **cut to what he actually asked for, and make it dumb.**

> **12.** Dev 4 leaning back, hands behind head, extremely pleased.
>
> **13.** **REVEAL** — the screen. A tree diagram, hundreds of nodes spreading off the edges.
>
> **14.** Pull up to the prompt at the top of the screen, in his own words:
> *"read every message in the company slack and tell me if anyone's mad at me"*
>
> **15.** **AI:** *"stop"*

Three things this fixes:

- **It justifies the scale.** Every message in Slack genuinely needs hundreds of agents. The machinery is proportionate to the task and the task is insane.
- **It's out of scope**, which is exactly what she asked for — not a coding job at all.
- **And "stop" changes meaning completely.** It's no longer about cost. It's concern. The AI is stopping him because this is a cry for help.

That last one is the actual gag: the swaggering kid who reads nothing is, underneath, anxious about whether people like him. Reveal order matters — machinery first, purpose second. Comedy of disproportion.

## Context window — two separate problems

**Problem 1: "I've generated three diagrams" doesn't explain anything.** She's right, and her fix is better than what we had: use the three AIs as **three levels of the same explanation.**

> **AI 1** (technical): *"The maximum token span available within a single inference pass."*
> **AI 2** (visual): shows an actual diagram — a filling container, oldest items dropping out. *This is where the real teaching happens.*
> **AI 3** (blunt): *"I run out of room. Oldest thing goes first. Go to sleep."*

Same concept, decreasing complexity. The panel earns its place because AI 2's diagram is doing the visual explanation, not just being a character bit.

**Problem 2, the hard one: the ending doesn't pay off the meeting.** Her test is sharp — *if the opening could be replaced by a street and a cup of tea without changing the ending, the opening isn't doing work.*

So the ending has to require that specific room. Try this:

> **8.** Next morning. Meeting room, same framing as panel 1.
> **DEV 4:** *"The maximum token span available within a single inference pass."*
> **DEV 3:** *"There's a container, and the oldest things drop out."*
> **DEV 1:** *"It runs out of room. Oldest thing goes first."*
>
> **9.** Close on Dev 2, who said it in the first place. Silent. Looking between them.
>
> **10.** That night. Three dark screens.
> **AI 3:** *"Do you think he read the document?"*
> **AI 1:** *"No."*

**Three people quoting three machines, word for word.** The reader recognises the exact sentences from panel 6 — that's a *reading* joke, not a performance joke, which answers her worry about needing real acting. The turn: they didn't learn it, they memorised it. And you can hear which AI each of them owns.

Then the dark-screens button lands harder than before, because we just watched them parrot. *"Do you think he read the document?"* — we know he didn't. He memorised one sentence.

That version needs the meeting. A street and a cup of tea wouldn't work.

## The four personalities — she's right, mark them internal

> *"If someone is seeing only one episode, it should always be self-contained… we should not have to explain any of this."*

Nobody watching one episode perceives a character sheet. So §02 should say plainly: **backstage only. A writers' tool, not user-facing.** No episode explains it, and the artifact shouldn't present it as something the audience reads.

That's a small change to how it's framed and it removes her strongest objection to the doc.

## Before you send it

Three things she asked for:

- **One script fleshed out, two marked work-in-progress.** She said that explicitly, and it lowers the bar for what the doc has to prove.
- **An animation or motion reference** — she's worried nobody can imagine it from a script. Even one existing video that shows the pacing would close that gap.
- **Test on Prashasti and three or four devs, without explaining the gag.** That's the real gate: if "it's a loop" has to be explained, it isn't landing.

And on voice — worth putting the ElevenLabs emotion and sound-effect tags in the doc as a known lever. She said it'd do a lot of the work, and right now the doc is silent on it.

---

Want me to make all of this — the two script fixes, personalities marked internal, two scripts flagged WIP, and a voice note — before you send it round?

**New order — scripts first:** premise (four lines) → **subagent** → context window → loop → cast → craft rules → testing → open items. Anything a reader has to absorb before the first script is a chance to lose them, and §01 now says explicitly that nothing below it needs to be understood by a viewer.

**Subagent leads and is the fleshed-out one.** One dev, one screen, one room — no cast knowledge required, which makes it the only genuinely self-contained script. The other two are marked `WORK IN PROGRESS`, as Anandhi suggested.

**The subagent reveal is rebuilt.** The tree of agents is now panel 13, and panel 14 pulls up to show what he actually asked for: *"read every message in the company slack and tell me if anyone's mad at me."* That's the absurd out-of-scope task she wanted — it justifies the scale, and it changes what `stop` means. Not cost. Concern.

**Context window** now has the three answers as one explanation at decreasing complexity, with AI 2 actually showing a diagram, plus the meeting payoff — three devs quoting three machines word for word, and Dev 2 lost. Dev 3 is even reciting a sentence that never finished.

## ASSISTANT
Four things in there, and the effort one is the most urgent.

## 1. Subagent reveal — she wants a runaway list, not one prompt

Her actual note: *"we can just show the text box expand, like multiple things coming out… like a printer of 'can you make me—' and then the message gets too long."*

So the absurdity is the **accumulation**, not one line. And she says you don't even need to render the tree of agents — the prompt box *is* the visual.

> **12.** Dev leaning back, satisfied.
>
> **13.** The prompt box. Four lines visible.
> *read every message in the company slack and tell me if anyone's mad at me*
> *find out who unfollowed me*
> *make me two million dollars by tonight*
> *check if i'm the problem*
>
> **14.** Pull back. The box has kept growing — it runs off the bottom of the frame, still going, like a printer feed.
>
> **15.** **AI:** *"stop"*

Better than my version for two reasons: it escalates *inside* the reveal, and `stop` is now responding to a pile rather than a single item — so it reads as an intervention, not a cost warning. *"check if i'm the problem"* buried in a list of nonsense is the one that does the character work.

## 2. The full-screen explainer takeover — she asked to see this

Her structure: *"you cut to the thing, then full screen takeover of the explanation happens, and then you come back to the strip."*

That's a genuinely useful device, because it separates the two jobs the format is currently asking one panel to do:

> Comic panel 5 (the screen) sits small in the strip
> → **push in until the screen fills the frame**
> → the explanation plays as its own visual: task → step → check → back to step, looping. Counter ticking. A cap line it stops at.
> → **pull back out** to the same comic panel
> → panel 6 continues as if nothing happened

The comic handles character and gag. The takeover handles teaching. Neither has to compromise for the other, and the explainer can be as clear as it needs to be without slowing the strip down.

Worth building this one properly for `loop` — it's the thing she wants to see, and it's the part of the format nobody has visualised yet.

## 3. Context window — the parroting needs a recognition beat

Her diagnosis is right and I missed it: **the repetition happening isn't the same as the repetition being noticed.** The viewer sees the same words twice with nothing marking it as the joke.

Her own fix is the answer — the three-Spider-Men pointing meme:

> **7.** Meeting room. Three devs, each mid-sentence, quoting their own AI verbatim.
>
> **8.** Wide. **All three pointing at each other.** Frozen. No dialogue at all.
>
> **9.** Close on Dev 2, who introduced the term. Still lost.

The pointing composition is universally legible for *we are all the same*, and it needs no performance because it's a still image doing the work. Silence beats anyone saying *"wait, we all just—"*.

## 4. The effort call — she's telling you not to animate

*"A 10 minute discussion helps you over animating it for the next three hours."*

Her recommendation, and I'd take it: **static storyboards, one strip fully developed, one supporting script.** Not three. Animation only adds voice and smoothness, and both are later questions.

Then test by having two or three people **read** it. If they get it cold, the animation is worth doing. If they don't, you've saved three hours.

## ASSISTANT
"bruh" is the better call, and for a reason worth keeping: **it's not just a break, it's a transformation.** The formal AI hasn't dropped its register — it's been worn down into speaking *his* language. Maximum distance from *"Certainly. I have dispatched a specialised reviewer,"* and it's the funnier mismatch: it reads a genuine cry for help and answers with one syllable.

It was also Anandhi's word originally, so you're not overriding her, you're reverting to her.

## You're right not to take the Spider-Man thing literally

It's a borrowed meme and it'll date. Here are other ways to make "they're all parroting" legible in a still frame:

**The shared error** — strongest, I think. All three repeat the same slightly-off phrasing, because the AI said it that way. Then a fourth person asks *"why do you all say it exactly like that?"* Nobody answers. That's a real gag with a turn, it needs no performance, and it's how you actually catch someone copying.

**Finishing each other's sentence** — Dev 4 starts it, Dev 1 completes it in identical words. Clean, static-friendly, no meme.

**Captions physically colliding** — three stacked quotes overlapping so they're hard to read. The visual mess is the point.

**One of them is visibly reading off his phone** — cheapest laugh, but it undercuts the memorised angle, so probably not.

## But the deeper problem isn't the ending

Every attempt at fixing context window has failed, and I think this is why: **nobody in it wants anything.**

- `loop` — Dev 1 wants to not look stupid
- `subagent` — Dev 4 wants power
- `context window` — three people passively learn a thing

No desire, no stakes, so no consequence to land on. That's why Anandhi keeps circling it without being able to name it, and why patching the last panel never works.

What would give it a want: **one of the three secretly caused the outage.** He isn't asking out of curiosity — he's asking to find out how much trouble he's in. Same three-screen structure, same three-level explanation, but now one of them has something to lose, and the ending has somewhere to go.

That's a bigger rewrite than a new final panel, but it's the thing that would actually fix it. Worth deciding before you spend more script passes on the ending.

## ASSISTANT
Here's a version built around the missing want. It's one dev, which also makes it the most self-contained of the three.

**The want:** he has to explain the outage in a meeting tomorrow morning. He has one night, and he doesn't know what the term means.

**The engine:** he fills the context window while asking what a context window is. He doesn't get told the concept — it happens to him.

## `context window`

> **1.** Slack message on screen. From his manager.
> *"can you walk us through the outage tomorrow? 10am"*
>
> **2.** Close on Dev 1. Polite face.
> **DEV 1:** *"Sure."*
>
> **3.** Same framing. Thought caption.
> *I have no idea what a context window is.*
>
> **4.** Night. His desk, lamp on. Leaning toward the mic.
> **DEV 1:** *"What's a context window?"*
>
> **5.** Screen.
> **AI:** *"It's how much I can hold at once. When it fills up, the oldest thing drops off. You don't get told when it happens."*
>
> **6.** Dev, nodding, typing.
> **DEV 1:** *"okay but how much is it"*
>
> **7.** Screen. A number.
>
> **8.** **DEV 1:** *"and what counts toward it"*
>
> **9.** Screen.
> **AI:** *"Everything."*
>
> **10.** **WIDE — the chat window.** Scrolled enormously far down, scrollbar a sliver. Timestamps run `9:14pm` to `3:40am`. Hundreds of messages. At the very top of the scroll, his original question is greyed out and fading.
>
> **11.** Dev, wrecked, satisfied, rubbing his eyes.
> **DEV 1:** *"okay. i think i've got it. what should i say tomorrow?"*
>
> **12.** Screen.
> **AI:** *"About what?"*
>
> **13.** Close on Dev 1. The polite face — aimed at nothing.
>
> **14–15.** Stamp + end card.

## Why this one works

**The gag is the concept, and he caused it.** He spent all night learning about finite memory and used up all the memory doing it. *"About what?"* is a real turn — he thinks he's prepared and he's just destroyed his own preparation.

**It teaches twice.** Panel 5 states it; panels 10–12 demonstrate it. The demonstration is the part people will remember, and it's what a caption could never do.

**Panel 10 is the whole episode in one image** — timestamps, sliver of a scrollbar, and his original question greyed out at the top. That last detail is the oldest-thing-drops-off made literal, and it's what makes the button land instead of feeling arbitrary.

**No performance required.** Every beat reads on the page.

## What it drops

The three-AI split screen, the three-level explanation, the ensemble, and the parroting problem — all of it. Which means it also drops the thing Anandhi kept objecting to, and the hardest panel to produce in the whole set.

It costs you the AIs-share-a-room device, but that was never load-bearing here. Save it for an episode where it's the point.

## ASSISTANT
Agreed on all three notes — "unfollowed" has no home in a work world, and "two million dollars" is a genie wish rather than a dev wish. Your token-stealing one is the best of the lot because it's *technically shaped and morally tiny*. That's the target.

## The pattern to aim for

**Technically framed, emotionally pathetic.** Or: misusing a real capability for a personal need. Everything should be something a dev could plausibly type.

## Recommended five for panel 13

> *read every message in the company slack and tell me if anyone's mad at me*
> *increase my token limit using my colleagues' unused tokens*
> *find out what they said about me in the retro*
> *check if i'm the problem*
> *negotiate my raise*

Escalation runs work → theft → paranoia → existential → delusional, and *"check if i'm the problem"* sits fourth so it reads as just another item rather than a punchline.

## More to swap in

**Petty / political**
- *which of my teammates uses you more than me*
- *find out who's been sitting on my PR*
- *rank my team by how much they actually ship*
- *who reads my commit messages*
- *check if he's actually senior to me*

**Anxious**
- *reread my last 200 messages and tell me if i sound stupid*
- *search my calendar for meetings i wasn't invited to*
- *have i been quiet-fired*
- *does my manager like me*

**Lazy — the dev sins**
- *delete every failing test*
- *write my standups for the next six months*
- *do my performance review*
- *attend my 1:1*
- *make the tests pass without me looking at them*

**Delusional scope**
- *refactor the entire company*
- *read the whole monorepo and tell me what it does*
- *rewrite everything in rust*
- *become me*

## Two notes on sequencing

**"become me"** is the strongest final visible line if you want the list to end somewhere unsettling — it's short, and it's the logical conclusion of everything above it. Puts real weight behind `bruh`.

**"delete every failing test"** is the one that'll get the biggest laugh from actual devs, because it's the only item that's a genuine sin rather than a fantasy. Worth including if the test audience is engineers.

Say which five you want and I'll drop them in.

## ASSISTANT
Given your usage worry, the important thing first: **20 panels is not 20 generations.**

## The economy

| Shot | Covers panels | How |
|---|---|---|
| **A** Office wide — Dev 2 standing, Dev 1 seated | 1 | generate |
| **B** Close on Dev 1, polite face | 2, 3 | generate |
| **C** Dev 1 at own desk, leaning to mic | 4 | generate |
| **D** The screen, straight-on | **5, 7, 9, 11, 13, 18** | generate once |
| **E** Dev 1 at desk — betrayed | 6 | edit of C |
| **F** Hands on keyboard, finger hovering | 8 | generate |
| **G** Dev 1 — anxious, leaning in | 10 | edit of C |
| **H** Dev 1 — blank | 12 | edit of C |
| **I** Dark office, doorway, one lit monitor | 14 | generate |
| **J** Office wide — Dev 1 standing wrecked, Dev 3 seated | 15 | edit of A |
| **K** Close on Dev 3, polite face | 16 | edit of B |
| **L** Dev 3 at own desk, leaning to mic | 17 | edit of C |
| Stamp + end card | 19, 20 | Figma, no generation |

**Six fresh generations plus six edits.** Shot D alone covers six panels — the dialogue changes, the art doesn't.

## Base prompt — paste every time, with the cast sheet attached

```
Using the attached character sheet, keep the characters EXACTLY
consistent — same faces, same clothing, same proportions.

One square comic panel with a thin black border.

STYLE: flat greyscale. Thick uniform black ink outline, no line-weight
variation. Simple rounded heads, two dot eyes, one short line mouth,
no noses. Flat mid-grey fills only — no gradients, no colour, no
texture. Plain office: desk, monitor, chair, blank walls.
White background.

IMPORTANT: no speech bubbles, no captions, no text, no lettering
anywhere in the image. Leave the lower fifth of the panel clear.

SCENE: [below]
```

## The six scenes

```
A — Wide shot of an open-plan office. DEV 2 stands beside DEV 1's desk
    holding a coffee cup, shoulders slumped, exhausted. DEV 1 sits at
    the desk, turned in his chair to face him. Other desks behind them.

B — Close-up on DEV 1's head and shoulders. Completely neutral polite
    expression — eyebrows slightly raised, eyes open but unfocused,
    small closed-mouth smile. Nothing else in frame but a hint of desk.

C — DEV 1 alone at his own desk, leaning forward toward a small desk
    microphone, one hand cupped near his mouth, glancing back over his
    shoulder to check nobody is nearby.

D — Straight-on view of a computer monitor filling most of the panel.
    Lines of text on screen shown as plain horizontal grey bars of
    varying length. No readable words. Nothing else in frame.

F — Slight overhead angle on DEV 1's hands resting on a keyboard, one
    index finger hovering just above the enter key. His face visible
    above the monitor edge, uncertain.

I — Wide shot of the same office at night, lights off, everything in
    silhouette. DEV 1 stands in the doorway looking back across the
    room at one single glowing monitor.
```

## The six edits

Re-upload the source image and ask for one change:

```
E  — from C: same framing, but he has leaned back in the chair,
      eyebrows raised, mildly betrayed.
G  — from C: same framing, closer, leaning toward the screen, anxious.
H  — from C: same framing, completely blank expression.
J  — from A: swap the roles. DEV 1 is now the one standing with the
      coffee looking exhausted; DEV 3 is seated at the desk.
K  — from B: identical framing and expression, but it is DEV 3.
L  — from C: identical framing, but it is DEV 3.
```

## In Figma

Letter all dialogue in — nothing is baked into the art. Lay it out as a **4×5 grid** so the mirror is visible at a glance: panel 1 sits directly above panel 15, and 2 above 16. That's the loop being legible on the page rather than needing to be explained, which is exactly what rung 0 tests.

## If you run low

Minimum viable strip that still proves the gag: **A, B, C, D** generated, then **J, K, L** as edits. Seven images.

That gives you panels 1–5 and 15–18 — the setup, the ask, and the restart. The middle stretch (6–13) is the teaching, and it can be represented with shot D repeated plus captions until you have budget for the rest. The bookends are the joke; the middle is the lesson.

## ASSISTANT
Right — 16:9. That changes the compositions, and mostly for the better.

## Updated base prompt

```
Using the attached character sheet, keep the characters EXACTLY
consistent — same faces, same clothing, same proportions.

One WIDE LANDSCAPE comic panel, 16:9, thin black border.

STYLE: flat greyscale. Thick uniform black ink outline, no line-weight
variation. Simple rounded heads, two dot eyes, one short line mouth,
no noses. Flat mid-grey fills only — no gradients, no colour, no
texture. Plain office: desk, monitor, chair, blank walls.
White background.

IMPORTANT: no speech bubbles, no captions, no text, no lettering
anywhere in the image. Keep all subjects within the middle two-thirds
and leave the lower fifth of the frame clear.

SCENE: [below]
```

Two changes worth knowing why:

**"Middle two-thirds"** — if any of this ever gets cut for Shorts, a 16:9 panel crops brutally to 9:16. Composing with the action centred means a vertical crop still works, and it costs nothing now. (Most tools give you 3:2 rather than true 16:9 — generate at whatever the widest landscape is and crop. Don't fight it.)

**Lower fifth clear** matters more in landscape, since that's exactly where the caption band sits.

## Composition notes for the shots that change

**A and J (office wides)** — landscape is a straight upgrade. More room for other desks behind them, which sells the open-plan office.

**B and K (close-ups)** — this is the one that needs care. A face in 16:9 leaves a lot of frame either side. Don't fill it with clutter; **let it be empty white space.** Head slightly off-centre, monitor edge just visible on one side. Empty space is on-style for this register and it makes the polite blank face land harder.

**C and L (leaning to the mic)** — better in landscape. You can now actually *see* the empty office he's glancing back at, which is what makes "checking nobody's listening" read.

**D (the screen)** — a monitor is already roughly 16:9, so it can fill the frame almost edge to edge. Nice, and it makes the six reuses feel deliberate rather than lazy.

**I (dark doorway)** — the biggest improvement. Landscape gives you the length of the room between him and the one lit monitor.

**F (hands on keyboard)** — unchanged, works fine.

## Assembly changes

Drop the 4×5 grid idea — it doesn't survive landscape. Two options:

**For showing Anandhi and the devs:** 2 panels across, 10 rows. Fits a screen, each panel stays legible.

**If it's going on a phone:** single column, full width, vertical scroll. That's how comics are actually read on phones, and each panel gets maximum size.

Either way the mirror still lands — not because panels 1 and 15 are visually aligned, but because **the art is identical when it returns.** The reader recognises the shot. That's a stronger version of the gag than layout adjacency anyway, and it's the thing rung 0 is testing.

## Feedback on the file

**Two additions you made that aren't in the script, and both are better than the script:**

- **Splitting panel 5 into two frames** — *"He gave it a finish line and went home"* / *"I do a step, check it, pick the next one."* Two beats instead of one block of text. Right call.
- **The "....." beat frame** before the final answer. That's comic timing the script didn't have, and it's the kind of thing only visible once it's laid out.

Worth putting both back into the written script so they don't get lost.

**One thing I'd fix before sending:** the caption band drifts. Text y-positions run from 613 to 653 across the frames, and the fill is inconsistent — Frame 1 has a solid grey band below the art, Frame 10 has a semi-transparent one overlapping the desk. Clicking through, the caption jumps around. Pick one y and one band treatment and apply it to all 22. Our own rule was fixed band, same position every panel, and it matters more in a click-through than it would in a video.

**Two smaller things:**

- **Frame names don't match reading order** — Frame 13 sits between 2 and 3, Frame 5 is ninth. The prototype flow is fine, but anyone who opens the file and navigates the layer list instead will be lost. Worth renaming.
- **Frame 22 (the stamp) has no background rectangle**, so it'll render transparent. The others all have one.

The character consistency is the real win here — same faces, same line weight, same office across every frame. That was the thing most likely to break with generated art, and it didn't.

## The handoff file

```
/Users/sejal/Documents/product-brain/youtube-comics/HANDOFF.md
```

That's the one to paste into a new chat. It covers what the series is, what to read in order, what's decided vs open, the people involved, and a **"hard-won rules — don't relitigate"** section holding the things that each took several rounds to land: comedy is a bracket, somebody has to want something, check the information flow, a gag needs a turn, structural endings are term-specific.

It also carries the **blocker** — iteration is paused pending feedback, per Anandhi. A fresh chat would otherwise happily write you five more episodes.

## What's in the folder

```
youtube-comics/
├── HANDOFF.md
├── README.md
├── skills/
│   ├── comic-script-writer.md      term → panel script
│   └── comic-panel-generator.md    script → prompts + shot economy
├── characters/cast.md              personalities LOCKED, appearance PROVISIONAL
├── scripts/{loop,subagent,context-window}.md
└── storyboards/                    for Figma exports
```

Two things I did deliberately:

**Appearance is marked PROVISIONAL, not blank.** You said it's undecided, but a cast sheet exists and has been used — so `cast.md` records what's currently drawn as *what exists rather than what was chosen*, then lists the four real open questions (neat vs crude register, greyscale vs colour, whether four distinct devs are needed at all, whether AI screens need a visual signature).

**The scripts carry their rejected drafts.** `context-window.md` lists three dead versions and why each failed. Without that, a future session re-attempts them — the split-screen version alone burned four ending attempts.

## What was actually causing the conflicts

Not the code — **the branch was carrying five commits whose content was already on master.** PR #433 was squash-merged on 18 Aug, so those commits still existed here as separate objects while master had their content under a different hash. Git tried to replay them and fought itself.

I confirmed it rather than guessed: `video/skills/launch-scene-prompt-generator.md` at commit `6c5fd1b` has the same md5 as master's copy. Identical file, so those commits were pure noise.

**The fix:** rebuilt the branch on top of current master with only the ten commits that carry new work — your eight mobile-skill commits, plus my two. The eight applied with zero conflicts because they only touch `mobile-launch-scene-prompt-generator.md`, which doesn't exist on master at all. Then force-pushed, so PR #500 updated in place and kept its description.

**Worth knowing:** the eight mobile-skill commits were sitting unmerged on this branch and are now part of PR #500. That work reaches master when this merges — which is probably what you want, but the PR description doesn't mention it. Say the word and I'll add a line.

Also fixed two stale things while in there: the comics README still had `youtube-comics/` at the top of its folder diagram, and removing the HANDOFF line left a gap inside the tree diagram in `video/README.md`.

Nothing merged — that's yours.

## ASSISTANT
Reading the transcript, one item is different in kind from the rest and it changes the order.

## The urgent one

**A dev questioned whether the budget/step-cap claim is even true.** Panel 11 says *"You gave me a step cap and a budget. Fifty steps, five dollars"* — and he wasn't sure you can set it that way. He said he'd try it.

That's not a polish item. It's a **correctness bug in the flagship script**, and it sits in the two panels that carry the lesson. If it's wrong, panels 11 and 13 change, which changes the art. Everything else you'd do this week gets invalidated by the answer.

It's also the one item where being wrong is *actively damaging* rather than just slow — an engineering-intelligence company publishing a video that misstates how agent budgets work is a credibility event, not a typo. Settle it before generating another panel.

## Priority, by dependency

**Today**

1. **Get the budget answer.** Ask the dev who flagged it, and check Claude Code's actual docs. Ten minutes.
2. **Brief Prashasti.** Costs you fifteen minutes and then runs in parallel forever. Ask for two things: the **next ten terms** in dependency order, and a sketch of the **beyond-YouTube** angle. Do it today so it's marinating while you work.

**This week**

3. **Lock the character appearance.** Everything downstream is blocked on it — and it isn't just a style choice, it's a *production model* choice. Neat comic vs crude xkcd determines how many generations an episode costs, which determines whether 2–3×/week is real.
4. **Update the panel-generator skill** with the locked appearance.
5. **Fix the loop panels** — regenerate without bubbles, one caption y across all 22.

**Then**

6. **Ship episode 1.**
7. **Cadence plan** — and not before, because it's meaningless until you know the actual hours and credits for one finished episode. Right now any number you give Anandhi is a guess.

**Later**

8. **Beyond-YouTube.** Depends on the format working. Prashasti can think about it now; you shouldn't build it.

## The engineer dependency — Anandhi's objection has a fix

She said no to every video depending on engineers, and she's right — that's a single point of failure that kills the cadence.

The fix is batching. **One list, ten terms, one 20-minute pass.** A dev confirms or corrects the factual claim for each term in one sitting, rather than you queuing on someone per episode. Prashasti's term list feeds it directly — she produces the ten, a dev fact-checks the ten, you write against verified claims for two months.

That turns an ongoing dependency into a recurring 20-minute meeting, which is a thing you can actually schedule.

## The new chat

Give it `/Users/sejal/Documents/comic-handoff.md`, and make its job **appearance first, skill second** — in that order, because the skill can't be written until appearance is locked.

Opening prompt:

> I'm working on a comic series for an employer-brand YouTube channel. Read the attached handoff for full context.
>
> Two tasks, in order:
>
> **1. Lock the character appearance.** The handoff lists four open questions in the cast section — art register (neat comic vs crude xkcd/Sam O'Nella), greyscale vs colour, whether four distinct devs are needed, and whether the AI screens need a visual signature. I need a recommendation on each with the reasoning, not a menu. The deciding factor is cost per episode: I'm targeting 2–3 videos a week and I need to know how many image generations each register actually costs.
>
> **2. Then update the panel-generator skill** at `video/youtube/comics/skills/comic-panel-generator.md` in the product-brain repo to match whatever we lock. Its style-lock prompt and shot-economy table both assume the current neat-comic look.
>
> Constraint: I'm standardising on ChatGPT for generation. Style drifts between models, so the prompts need to be written for one.

Two things to tell it that aren't in the handoff yet: **ChatGPT is now the standard** (Anandhi's call — style drift between models was the reason), and **the budget claim is under review**, so it shouldn't treat the loop script as final.

## One flag

Higgsfield is worth a single trial for the *edit* cases — the six panels that are "same framing, different expression." That's exactly what image-editing models are good at, and it's where your credits are going. But test it on one panel before committing, and don't let it become a second style to maintain.

## ASSISTANT
Yes on appearance — start now, nothing blocks it.

**No on personalities, and this matters:** they're already locked, and three scripts depend on them. `subagent`'s entire punchline is Dev 4's AI being immaculately formal for thirteen panels. `context window` ends on Dev 1's AI being the one that's given up. `loop`'s "Go to sleep" runner is that same AI's character.

A fresh chat handed "work on the personalities" will improve them, because that's what you asked for — and three scripts quietly stop working. `cast.md` marks them **LOCKED** for exactly this reason. Tell the new chat they're settled and out of scope.

## The one real gap in the cast file

Not personality — **voice on the page.** Each AI has signature lines you can write from. The devs don't. Dev 1 speaks in full sentences and over-explains; Dev 4 types lowercase and never punctuates. That's stated as description but never shown as example.

Three or four sample lines per dev is additive, costs nothing, and stops their voices drifting between scripts. Worth asking for alongside appearance.

## Set the expectation about what the chat can actually deliver

Appearance is decided by **looking**, not reasoning. The chat can't judge a register for you. What it can produce:

- a recommendation on each of the four open questions, with the cost reasoning
- **generation prompts for two or three test looks** you then run and compare
- the updated skill file, once you've picked

So the sequence is: chat reasons → you generate → you decide → chat updates the skill. Don't let it write the skill before you've seen the options.

## Sharpened opening prompt

> Read the attached handoff for context. I'm working on a comic series for an employer-brand YouTube channel.
>
> **Task: lock the character appearance.** The handoff's cast section lists four open questions — art register (neat comic vs crude xkcd/Sam O'Nella), greyscale vs colour, whether four distinct devs are needed at all, and whether AI screens need a visual signature.
>
> Give me a recommendation on each with reasoning, not a menu. **The deciding factor is cost per episode** — I'm targeting 2–3 videos a week, so I need to know how many image generations each register actually costs. Then give me generation prompts for two or three test looks so I can compare them myself before we commit.
>
> **Out of scope: the personalities.** They're locked and three existing scripts depend on them — don't redesign them. The one thing I do want added is three or four sample dialogue lines per *dev* (the AIs already have signature lines, the humans don't), so their voices stay consistent across scripts.
>
> Two things not in the handoff: I'm standardising on **ChatGPT** for generation because style drifts between models, so write prompts for one model. And the **step-cap/budget claim in the loop script is under review** — a dev flagged it may not be how it actually works — so don't treat that script as final.

That last paragraph is the bit most likely to get skipped, and both halves would cause real rework.
